She played it in her memory, because the violin was back at the watchtower and she could not bring herself to leave him long enough to retrieve it. She played it in her memory, and the glass answered. The song leaked from the shard — faint, fractured, but real. He was still there. Not breathing. Not speaking. Not grinning at her with that impossible certainty. But present: the low cello line, steady and warm, grounding her the way he had always grounded her.

The gap between knowing he was gone and feeling that she was still real — that was the [[Void]] opening. The shadow of what she had lost. The space it left behind.

And in that space, something crystallized.

His dream still existed. She was still its keeper. And now the seed was with her.

The song in the glass was proof, and through her crying she gave the ache a direction, the way she had always done, by turning it into a vow:

_"I wish I had the power to transform all of this agonizing loss into anything that would, one day, make real the title of my greatest knight."_

Where her last tears fell on the saber blade driven half-deep into the soil, her [[Soul Leitmotif]] crystallized as [[Void]] — deep purple, shot through with black veins like lightning frozen in glass, the same shade as the violet of the tree they had dreamed of, darkened by the shadow of his absence.

She did not sleep that night. She sat against the oak with the [[Sky Glass]] shard in her hands and listened to the song until the sun rose and the sky turned the color of old bruises and the world kept turning without asking her permission.

She retrieved the [[Resonance Box]] from where they had dropped it at the courtyard's edge. It was undamaged. She did not play it. She could not. She strapped it to her pack and carried it with her to the oak.

Before she left the clearing, she knelt at the grave one final time.

"I will plant it," she said. "At the center. I promise."

She wore the circlet's empty copper frame on her wrist from that day forward, right below her [[Soul Leitmotif]]. 

The [[Sky Glass]] shard was in her pocket, warm against her hip. The circlet itself — the whole of it, bent and empty — rested on the blade of the saber's hilt beneath the oak.

And the seed, the small dark seed that had crossed a continent in a boy's pocket, hung beside the copper wire, waiting for the ground it had been promised, resting as if mirroring the pulse of [[Void]] gem that flickered with her own heartbeat.

Whenever the world threatened to drown her, she touched the [[Sky Glass]] of her hips, and heard him through his cello: _You are a princess. One day you will be an empress._

She had never quite believed the princess part.

But she had believed him believing it.

The last thing he gave her was weight,

But it wasn't purely cold weight,

It was also warmth,

And that warmth, was everything, for it is love.

Weight of his hand enveloped in warmth.

And that was close enough.

For singers remember.

### Draft Score 3: The Violet in the [[Waltz of Wandering Love]]

#### Act 13

_"That was close enough,"_

I repeat while closing my eyes.

_"For singers remember."_

A tear goes down my cheek.

_"When the living cannot."_

The stage is quiet.

My throat hurts. I always forget that part when I imagine telling it.

_"I asked the [[Auric Aria]] for [[Fated Flower]]s. She gave me rain."_

I say to myself one last time.

I don't know how long I've been standing here. The fountain behind me is still running — the seal broke sometime in the second act and no one thought to stop it.

The story of [[Artus]] and the girl from the caravan had a small, indifferent water sound beneath it, which is perhaps more honest than silence would have been. The amphitheater smells like torch smoke and the particular warmth of a crowd that has been still for a long time.

That is how I tell it. The parts I could not know — what he saw, what he chose to believe in the last second, why he froze in the fight — I imagined faithfully. [[Ballad]]s are allowed that. They are only not allowed to lie about the weight.

And I'm truthful, because everything else certainly happened.

I am still here, and now there's the [[Void]] of silence in front of me.

Then someone in the left gallery breaks it.

The sound builds the way it always surprises me — not all at once, but in a wave, one person deciding and then two, and then the part of any crowd that was waiting for permission. By the time it reaches full voice the amphitheater is loud in a way the granary walls were not designed for. Someone in the upper tier is stamping their feet. A woman near the front is pressing both hands to her mouth in the way people do when they don't know what else to do with feeling.

And somewhere in the middle of it, the chant begins.

It starts as a handful of voices, rhythmic, insistent — the particular cadence of a crowd that has found a name and wants to use it:

_Violet! — Violet! — Violet! —_

I open my eyes.

I have heard it before. Three cities, two seasons, and the travelling circuit that has taken me through several [[Enclave]]s for passage into the northern plains while paying in marks. The name arrived the way stage names always arrive — not from me, but from an audience who needed something to call the thing they had felt, and gave it back to me as a gift I never asked for and could not refuse.

Violet. The Starlight Actress.

I lower the bow of the violin.

I do not bow immediately. I never bow immediately. There is a breath I take — one, counted, the way Maret taught me to count marks: without panic, as a tool — and in that breath I am no one's Violet Actress. I am a woman standing on a borrowed stage in a granary repurposed by ambition, with a throat that hurts and a pouch that is about to be lighter than I'd like, with a [[Sky Glass]] shard warm against my hip.

Then I take a bow.

The chant doesn't stop. If anything it sharpens — _Violet!, Violet!_ — and a young man near the aisle has stood up and is looking at me the way people look at something they are trying to memorize, as though I might be taken from them before they finish.

He doesn't know my name.

None of them do.

They know the girl from the [[Ballad]]. They know the one who sat in the cave mouth with the fires in the valley. They know [[Artus]], the seed, the copper circlet and the [[Sky Glass]], and they have wept for the boy beneath the oak because the story leaves no other honest choice — I have made sure of that, night after night, [[Enclave]] after [[Enclave]], learning precisely which words carry the most weight and where to place the silence.

But they do not know that the girl they wept for is the woman holding the violin.

They do not know that the circlet on my wrist is not a costume piece.

They do not know my name is [[Amadea]].

Their [[Amadea]] is a [[Legend]]. Allegedly, an empress from a [[Ballad]] so old it has no confirmed origin. A woman travelling from another version of the [[Great Plague]], or after it, or during it — the story shifts depending on which version you find, and there are many, because names with that kind of weight accumulate stories the way rivers accumulate silt.

They would not recognize the connection. Why would they? The girl who ran with caravans and stole bread and sat in dry riverbeds crying over bent copper wire does not look like a [[Legend]]. She looks like a working musician in a coat that has been mended at both elbows, holding a violin that is good but not remarkable, who tells an old [[Ballad]] very well and has a stage name that someone else invented.

Violet, the Starlight Violinist.

It is a good name. I will admit that freely. Whoever first called me that had an instinct for the image — the violet, the performance, the distance between what is shown and what is carried. It is a name that tells the truth without explaining it.

And it keeps the other name safe.

Safe for what, I am not yet certain.

But I am not ready for it to be recognized. Not here. Not in a granary in Vesser's Cross with a leaking fountain and twenty-three marks and a settlement that exists only as a seed in a leather pouch and a direction of the cold trail I'm following.

The applause begins to settle. The chant fades in the way chants fade — unevenly, the most enthusiastic voices last. The young man near the aisle is still standing. The woman near the front has lowered her hands from her mouth.

A hunched critic in the fourth row is wiping his snot with a cloth much more expensive than the violin in my hands.

"That was beautiful," He says.

I step down from the platform.

The amphitheater is doing what amphitheaters do after a performance — exhaling. People find their coats. Conversations restart. The particular spell of collective stillness breaks into its component parts, which are just ordinary people remembering they have somewhere to be. The young man near the aisle is talking to someone beside him now, animated, gesturing in the way people gesture when they are trying to describe something they felt but can't quite locate in language.

The critic has not moved.

He has the patience of a man who considers waiting a form of authority. He watches me pack up with the expression of someone composing his opinion while I do it — the slow bow-loosening, the violin into the case, the clasps closing one, two — and I can feel the opinion forming the way you feel weather changing, not with dread, just with the particular awareness that something is about to land on you.

I carry the case off the platform's edge. The critic angles toward me with the practiced drift of someone who has cornered performers in many corridors.

He falls into step just close enough that I cannot pretend not to notice him.

"The story," He says, when I am close enough. "The boy. The knight. Is it traditional? I don't recognize the [[Ballad]]."

I keep walking at the same pace.

"It's an old one," I say. "Eastern. Before the [[Great Plague]] made everyone forget everything."

"Hm." He considers this with the particular hm of someone who suspects they are being given a partial truth and is deciding whether to pursue it. "The structure is unusual. The boy is more fully realized than the girl, for most of it. Normally the central figure commands more — presence. More interiority."

"She's seven," I say. "Then twelve. Then fifteen. Her interiority is grief. It doesn't need more presence than that."

"Still." He tilts his head. The cloth reappears. "There's a craft argument that the protagonist should anchor the audience's sympathy more directly, and actually there aren't any other major characters during [[Amadea]]'s childhood, this is a big oversight! Instead the knight takes up considerable—"

"He's supposed to," I say.

He blinks. I have interrupted him more cleanly than he expected.

"The point," I continue, still walking, "is that she doesn't know she's the protagonist yet. She thinks the story is about him. That's not a structural weakness. That's the entire play viewed from inside someone [[Grief-Stricken]]. The static is precisely why she doesn't name anyone else. Don't tell me you didn't catch something that subtle."

A pause. He is visibly thinking of a rebuttal but frustrated that he can't think of one.

Moreover, we have reached the side corridor that leads to the exit. The torches here have burned lower and the smoke smell is stronger. I can see the door at the end and the dark rectangle of the night beyond it.

"But the ending still felt unfinished," he claims, trying a different angle. "The girl doesn't become anything. It's a tragedy that ends in a burial—"

I stop.

Not for him — or not only for him. I stop because the door is there and I want to go through it, and I find that I want to say this first.

I turn to face him.

He is smaller than he seemed from the stage. Most things are.

"It hasn't finished yet," I say.

He opens his mouth.

"The [[Ballad]]," I clarify, pleasantly. "It's the first act of an ongoing larger [[Magnum Opus]]. That's the nature of it. The girl is still walking, finding meaning in the loss."

He looks at me with the expression of a man encountering a sentence he has decided must be metaphor and is waiting for it to resolve into one.

"Then how does the audience know it's over?" he asks.

I pick up the case.

"They don't," I say. "That's part of the charm of a story that hasn't ended. You feel the edge, not the wall. And If you want to know more, I invite you to attend to my next presentation three [[Moon]]s from now."

I go through the door.

The night air in Vesser's Cross smells like canal water and cooling stone. I stand in the alley beside the granary and press my back against the wall and breathe.

The applause is still faintly audible through the granary's side wall — or I'm imagining it, which amounts to the same thing. The body keeps the sound for a while after. That's the part they don't tell you about performing: you carry the crowd home with you and you have to wait for it to drain.

I tilt my head back against the stone.

Above the alley, between the rooflines, a strip of sky. The stars are doing what stars do — indifferently present, recursive, patient. The Sky Glass shard is warm against my hip. I don't take it out. I just hold it through the fabric for a moment and let the cello line come, low and steady, the way it always comes.

Still there.

The critic's question drifts back: _The girl doesn't become anything._

I consider this in the dark, with the canal smell and the last ember of applause, and find that it does not make me angry. It doesn't even make me sad. It makes me something more specific — the feeling of standing at the beginning of something enormous and being the only one who can see it from where you're standing.

He saw a story that stopped.

I am standing inside a story that hasn't.

I push off the wall.

Twenty-three marks. The inn will take eight. The road east will take three in tolls if the checkpoint guards are honest and six if they're not. There is another [[Indulgent Enclave]] three days east with an actual stage and a contracted audience and a steward who has already sent word that the Violet Actress is expected.

They will chant the name again. They will not know what it protects.

The seed is in my coat pocket. The city is still east of something I haven't found yet but should be close to the reach of my real destination.

I pick up the case and walk to sleep.

I need the energy. The next crossing will be through an [[Age of Embers]] waste.
